# BASIC DIFFERENTIAL EQUATIONS and SOLUTIONS

| DIFFERENTIAL EQUATION                                                                                   | SOLUTION                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 18.1 Separation of variables $f_1(x) g_1(y) dx + f_2(x) g_2(y) dy = 0$                                  | $\int \frac{f_1(x)}{f_2(x)} dx + \int \frac{g_2(y)}{g_1(y)} dy = c$                                                                                                                                                      |
| 18.2 Linear first order equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)$                                         | $ye^{\int P dx} = \int Qe^{\int P dx} dx + c$                                                                                                                                                                            |
| 18.3 Bernoulli's equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)y^n$                                             | $ve^{(1-n)\int Pdx}=(1-n)\int Qe^{(1-n)\int Pdx}dx+c$ where $v=y^{1-n}$ . If $n=1$ , the solution is $\ln y=\int (Q-P)dx+c$                                                                                              |
| 18.4 Exact equation $M(x,y) dx + N(x,y) dy = 0$ where $\partial M/\partial y = \partial N/\partial x$ . | $\int M  \partial x  +  \int \left( N  -  \frac{\partial}{\partial y}  \int M  \partial x \right) dy = c$ where $\partial x$ indicates that the integration is to be performed with respect to $x$ keeping $y$ constant. |
| 18.5 Homogeneous equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = F\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)$                                   | $\ln x = \int \frac{dv}{F(v)-v} + c$ where $v=y/x$ . If $F(v)=v$ , the solution is $y=cx$ .                                                                                                                              |

| DIFFERENTIAL EQUATION                                                                                                       | SOLUTION                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 18.6 $y F(xy) dx + x G(xy) dy = 0$                                                                                          | $\ln x = \int \frac{G(v)  dv}{v\{G(v) - F(v)\}} + c$ where $v = xy$ . If $G(v) = F(v)$ , the solution is $xy = c$ .                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| 18.7 Linear, homogeneous second order equation                                                                              | Let $m_1, m_2$ be the roots of $m^2 + am + b = 0$ . Then there are 3 cases.  Case 1. $m_1, m_2$ real and distinct:                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + a\frac{dy}{dx} + by = 0$ a, b are real constants.                                                      | $y = c_1 e^{m_1 x} + c_2 e^{m_2 x}$ Case 2. $m_1, m_2$ real and equal: $y = c_1 e^{m_1 x} + c_2 x e^{m_1 x}$ Case 3. $m_1 = p + qi, \ m_2 = p - qi$ : $y = e^{px} (c_1 \cos qx + c_2 \sin qx)$ where $p = -a/2, \ q = \sqrt{b - a^2/4}$ .                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| 18.8 Linear, nonhomogeneous second order equation $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + a\frac{dy}{dx} + by = R(x)$ a, b are real constants. | There are 3 cases corresponding to those of entry 18.7 above.  Case 1. $y = c_1 e^{m_1 x} + c_2 e^{m_2 x}$ $+ \frac{e^{m_1 x}}{m_1 - m_2} \int e^{-m_1 x} R(x) dx$ $+ \frac{e^{m_2 x}}{m_2 - m_1} \int e^{-m_2 x} R(x) dx$ Case 2. $y = c_1 e^{m_1 x} + c_2 x e^{m_1 x}$ $+ x e^{m_1 x} \int e^{-m_1 x} R(x) dx$ $- e^{m_1 x} \int x e^{-m_1 x} R(x) dx$ Case 3. $y = e^{px} (c_1 \cos qx + c_2 \sin qx)$ $+ \frac{e^{px} \sin qx}{q} \int e^{-px} R(x) \cos qx dx$ $- \frac{e^{px} \cos qx}{q} \int e^{-px} R(x) \sin qx dx$ |
| 18.9 Euler or Cauchy equation $x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + ax \frac{dy}{dx} + by = S(x)$                                        | Putting $x = e^t$ , the equation becomes $\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + (a-1)\frac{dy}{dt} + by = S(e^t)$ and can then be solved as in entries 18.7 and 18.8 above.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |

|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 18.10 Bessel's equation                                                                                                   |                                                                                                                                                                    |
| $x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + x \frac{dy}{dx} + (\lambda^2 x^2 - n^2)y = 0$                                                    | $y = c_1 J_n(\lambda x) + c_2 Y_n(x)$ See pages 136-137.                                                                                                           |
| 18.11 Transformed Bessel's equation $x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + (2p+1)x \frac{dy}{dx} + (\alpha^2 x^{2\tau} + \beta^2)y = 0$ | $y = x^{-p} \left\{ c_1 J_{q/r} \left( \frac{\alpha}{r} x^r \right) + c_2 Y_{q/r} \left( \frac{\alpha}{r} x^r \right) \right\}$ where $q = \sqrt{p^2 - \beta^2}$ . |
| 18.12 Legendre's equation $(1-x^2)\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}-2x\frac{dy}{dx}+n(n+1)y = 0$                                          | $y = c_1 P_n(x) + c_2 Q_n(x)$<br>See pages 146-148.                                                                                                                |

12.3 sets of points in the complex plane

- · Terminology
- Before discussing the concept of functions of a complex variable, we need to introduce some essential terminology about sets in the complex plane

Suppose  $Z_0 = x_0 + \overline{1}y_0$ Since  $|Z-Z_0| = \overline{(x-x_0)^2 + (y-y_0)^2}$  is the distance between the points  $Z=x+\overline{1}y$  and  $Z_0=x_0+\overline{1}y_0$ , the points  $Z=x+\overline{1}y$  that satisfy the equation

\$>0 lie on a circle of radius p centered at the point 20

![](_page_250_Picture_6.jpeg)

- · | Z-Z0 | = p
- · | Z Zo | < f : open airde or open diek
- $|z-z_0| \le c$  : closed circle or closed disk
- An open disk  $|Z-Z_0| < \ell$  is also called neighborhood of  $Z_0$  A point  $Z_0$  is said to be an interior point of a set Sof complex plane if there exists some neighborhood of  $Z_0$  If every point Z of a set S is an interior point,
  then S is said to be an open set.

open annulus :  $e_1 < |z-a| < e_2$ closed annulus :  $e_1 \le |z-a| \le e_2$ 

The boundary of a set S is the set of all boundary points of S  $|Z-\bar{1}| \leq 2$   $\longrightarrow$  boundary points  $|Z-\bar{1}| = 2$ 

If any poir of points 21 and 22 in an open set S can be connected by a polygonal line that lies entirely in the set, the open set is said to be connected.

- An open connected set is a complex, domain.
- The complement of a set S in the complex plane is the set of all points of the complex plane that do not belong to S.

## \* Rules of differentiation

constant mle :  $\frac{d}{dz} C = 0$ ,  $\frac{d}{dz} cf(z) = cf'(z)$ 

aun rule:  $\frac{d}{dz} \left[ f(z) + g(z) \right] = f(z) + g'(z)$ 

product rule =  $\frac{d}{dz} \left[ f(z) g(z) \right] = f(z) g'(z) + f(z) g(z)$ 

Audient rule:  $\frac{d}{dz} \left[ \frac{f(z)}{g(z)} \right] = \frac{g(z)f'(z) - f(z)g'(z)}{[g(z)]^2}$ 

Chain rule:  $\frac{d}{dz} f(g(z)) = f'(g(z)) g'(z)$ 

the usual rule for differentiation of powers of Z is also valid.

 $\frac{d}{dz}z^n = nz^{n-1}$ , n is an integer.

Definition: Analyticity at a joint

A complex function  $\omega = f(z)$  is said to be analytic at a point  $z_0$ . if f is differentiable at  $z_0$  and at every point in some neighborhood of  $z_0$ .

12.5 Candry - Riemann Equation

Theorem: Cauchy-kiemann Equation

: Suppose f(z) = u(x,y) + i v(x,y) is Continuous in some neighborhood of the point z = x + iy and is differentiable at z. Then at the point z the first order partial derivatives of u and v exist and satisfy the cauchy-Riemann egyptions

$$\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}$$
  $\Rightarrow \frac{\partial u}{\partial y} = -\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}$ 

· Proof

Since f'(Z) exists, we know that

$$f'(z) = \lim_{z \to \infty} f(z+zz) - f(z)$$

By writing  $f(z) = u(x,y) + \overline{v}(x,y)$  and  $\Delta z = \Delta x + \overline{v}(x,y)$  we get from

$$f(z) = \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{u(\alpha+s\alpha,y+sy)-iv(\alpha+s\alpha,y+sy)-u(\alpha,y)}{s\alpha+isy}$$

Gince this limits exists,  $\Delta Z$  can approach zero from any convenient direction. In particular, if  $\Delta Z \to 0$  horizontally, then  $\Delta Z = \Delta X$  and so

$$f'(z) = \lim_{\Delta z \to 0} \frac{u(\alpha_1 \alpha_2, y) - u(\alpha_1 y)}{\Delta x} + \int \lim_{\Delta z \to 0} \frac{v(\alpha_1 \alpha_2, y) - v(\alpha_1 y)}{\Delta x}$$

$$= \frac{\partial u}{\partial x} + \int \frac{\partial v}{\partial x} dx$$

Now, if let 22-90 vertically, then DZ= Tay

$$f'(z) = \lim_{z \to 0} \frac{u(z, y + \Delta y) - u(x, y)}{T\Delta y} + \frac{v(x, y + \Delta y) - v(x, y)}{T\Delta y}$$

$$= -i \frac{\partial y}{\partial n} + \frac{\partial y}{\partial n}$$

A linear fractional transformation is a complex function of the form

$$T(Z) = \frac{\partial Z + b}{CZ + d}$$

$$T'(Z) = \frac{\partial A - bC}{(CZ + d)^{2}} \rightarrow T = conformal & at Z$$

$$\int_{c} (x^{2} + iy^{2}) dz = \int_{1}^{2} (x + iy^{2}) dy = -\int_{1}^{2} y^{2} dy + i \int_{1}^{2} dy$$

$$= -\frac{1}{3} + i$$

$$\int_{c} (x^{2} + iy^{2}) dz = \frac{1}{3}i + (-\frac{1}{3} + i) = -\frac{1}{3} + \frac{1}{3}i$$

\* Granlation and Net Flux

Let T and N denote the unit tangent vector and the unit normal vector in a positively oriented simple closed contour C.

$$f(z) = u(x,y) + \pi(x,y)$$

$$\oint_C f \cdot N ds = \oint_C u dy - v dx$$

$$(f \cdot Tds) + T(f \cdot F \cdot Nds) = f \cdot (w - v) \cdot cdu + Tdy) = f \cdot f \cdot \overline{F} \cdot dz$$
  
 $circulation = Re \left[ f \cdot F(z) \cdot dz \right]$
